{\mathbf{z}}=({\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{\top},{\mathbf{z}}_{2}^{\top})^{\top}
, where
{\mathbf{z}}_{1}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{m})
and
{\mathbf{z}}_{2}=(\tilde{z}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{z}_{n})
are independent random vectors each with i.i.d. entries satisfying
{\mathbb{E}}[z_{1}]={\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{z}_{1}]=0
,
{\mathbb{E}}[z_{1}^{2}]={\mathbb{E}}[\tilde{z}_{1}^{2}]=1
,
\nu_{q}:={\mathbb{E}}|z_{1}|^{q}<\infty
and
\tilde{\nu}_{q}:={\mathbb{E}}|\tilde{z}_{1}|^{q}<\infty

for some
q\in[1,\infty)
.

a)

Let
C
be a deterministic
m\times n
matrix, then

\displaystyle{\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{\top}C{\mathbf{z}}_{2}=O_{L^{q}}(\lVert{C}\rVert_{F}),

where the constant in the estimate depends only on
q
and
\nu_{q},\widetilde{\nu}_{q}
.

b)

Let
M
be a deterministic
(m+n)\times(m+n)
matrix, then

\displaystyle{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}M{\mathbf{z}}-\mathrm{tr}M=O_{L^{q}}(\lVert{M}\rVert_{F}),

where the constant in the estimate depends only on
q
and
\nu_{k},\widetilde{\nu}_{k}
for
k\leq 2q
.

Proof.

a
)
By Lemma 2.2 and Lemma 2.3 of
Bai and
Silverstein 1998
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|{\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{\top}C{\mathbf{z}}_{2}\big|^{q}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\big|\sum_{i,j}z_{i}\widetilde{z}_{j}C_{ij}\big|^{q}\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}\big|\sum_{i,j}z_{i}^{2}\widetilde{z}_{j}^{2}C_{ij}^{2}\big|^{q/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(\sum_{i,j}{\mathbb{E}}[z_{i}^{2}\widetilde{z}_{j}^{2}C_{ij}^{2}]\big)^{q/2}+\sum_{i,j}{\mathbb{E}}[|z_{i}|^{q}|\widetilde{z}_{j}|^{q}|C_{ij}|^{q}]

\displaystyle=\big(\sum_{i,j}M_{ij}^{2}\big)^{q/2}+\nu_{q}\widetilde{\nu}_{q}\sum_{i,j}|C_{ij}|^{q}\leq(1+\nu_{q}\widetilde{\nu}_{q})\lVert{C}\rVert_{F}^{q},

where the last inequality holds since
\sum|C_{ij}|^{q}\leq(\sum|C_{ij}|^{2})^{q/2}
for
q\geq 2
.

b
)
Write
M=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}

where
A,B,C,D
are of dimensions such that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}M{\mathbf{z}}={\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{\top}A{\mathbf{z}}_{1}+{\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{\top}B{\mathbf{z}}_{2}+{\mathbf{z}}_{2}^{\top}C{\mathbf{z}}_{1}+{\mathbf{z}}_{2}^{\top}D{\mathbf{z}}_{2}.

By Lemma 2.7 of
Bai and
Silverstein 1998
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|{\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{\top}A{\mathbf{z}}_{1}-\mathrm{tr}A\big|^{q}\lesssim(\nu_{4}^{q/2}+\nu_{2q})\mathrm{tr}(AA^{\top})^{q/2}\leq(\nu_{4}^{q/2}+\nu_{2q})\lVert{M}\rVert_{F}^{q},

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|{\mathbf{z}}_{2}^{\top}D{\mathbf{z}}_{2}-\mathrm{tr}D\big|^{q}\lesssim(\tilde{\nu}_{4}^{q/2}+\tilde{\nu}_{2q})\mathrm{tr}(DD^{\top})^{q/2}\leq(\nu_{4}^{q/2}+\nu_{2q})\lVert{M}\rVert_{F}^{q}.

Then we can write

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|{\mathbf{z}}^{\top}M{\mathbf{z}}-\mathrm{tr}M|^{q}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}\big|{\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{\top}A{\mathbf{z}}_{1}-\mathrm{tr}A\big|^{q}+{\mathbb{E}}\big|{\mathbf{z}}_{2}^{\top}B{\mathbf{z}}_{2}-\mathrm{tr}B\big|^{q}

\displaystyle\quad+{\mathbb{E}}\big|{\mathbf{z}}_{1}^{\top}C{\mathbf{z}}_{2}\big|^{q}+{\mathbb{E}}\big|{\mathbf{z}}_{2}^{\top}D{\mathbf{z}}_{1}\big|^{q}.

and the claim follows from (
a
) of the lemma.
∎

Using Lemma
3
and Lemma
4
we can derive the following concentration inequalities for quadratic forms involving certain high probability events.

Lemma 5
.

Let
{\mathbf{x}},{\mathbf{f}},\bm{\epsilon}
be defined as in (
2.9
) and
q\leq 2
. Under Assumptions
1
and either Assumptions
2
or
3
we have

a)

For any (deterministic) square matrix
B
of size
T-\tau
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{\top}B{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}-{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{\top}B{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}]=O_{L^{q}}\left({\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}{\sqrt{T}}\lVert{B}\rVert\right),

where the expectation is satisfies

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{\top}B{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}]=1_{i=j}\mathrm{tr}\big(\Psi_{1}^{ii}(B)\big)=1_{i=j}O(\sigma_{i}^{2}T\lVert{B}\rVert).

b)

For all
i,j
we have

{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{f}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{\top}B\bm{\epsilon}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}]=0

and

\displaystyle{\mathbf{f}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{\top}B\bm{\epsilon}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}=O_{L^{2q}}(\sigma_{i}\sqrt{T}\lVert{B}\rVert).

c)

Suppose
n\in\{1,\ldots,K\}
and
c_{1},c_{2}
are positive constants with
c_{1}<c_{2}
. Pick any

\displaystyle a\in\left[c_{1},c_{2}\right]\mu_{n,\tau}^{2}.

Recall from (
2.11
) the resolvent
R(a):=(I_{T-\tau}-a^{-1}E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}E^{\top}E)^{-1}
, then

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}R(a)^{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}R(a)^{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]=O_{L^{q}}(\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sqrt{T}),

for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
where
\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[\ \cdot\ ]:={\mathbb{E}}[\ \cdot\ |{\mathcal{F}}_{p}]
is defined in (
2.23
). In particular,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}R(a)^{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}R(a)^{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]=O_{p}(\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sqrt{T}).

d)

Parts (
a
)-(
c
) of the lemma remain true if the vector
{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}
is replaced by
{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[1:T-\tau]}
and the operator
\Psi^{ii}_{1}
is replaced by
\Psi^{ii}_{0}
.

Proof.

a
)
We apply the truncation procedure as described in (
C.3a
).
Recalling (
C.3a
), (
C.3d
) and (
C.4
) we may write

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{(L)\top}B{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}^{(L)}

\displaystyle=(\sigma_{i}{\mathbf{f}}_{i,[1:T]}^{(L)}+\bm{\epsilon}_{i,[1:T]})^{\top}\begin{pmatrix}I_{T-\tau}\\
{\mathbf{0}}\end{pmatrix}B\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{0}}\\
I_{T-\tau}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}(\sigma_{j}{\mathbf{f}}_{j,[1:T]}^{(L)}+\bm{\epsilon}_{j,[1:T]})

\displaystyle=({\mathbf{z}}_{i,[1-L:T]}^{\top},\bm{\epsilon}_{i,[1:T]}^{\top})(\Psi_{1}^{ij}(B))({\mathbf{z}}_{i,[1-L:T]}^{\top},\bm{\epsilon}_{i,[1:T]}^{\top})^{\top}.

Applying (
b
) of Lemma
4
to the above quadratic form gives

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{(L)\top}B{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}^{(L)}-{\mathbb{E}}\left[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{(L)\top}B{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}^{(L)}\right]\right|^{q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lVert{\Psi^{ij}_{1}(B)}\rVert_{F}^{q},

(C.5)

where
{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T]}^{(L)\top}B{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[1:T]}^{(L)}]=1_{i=j}\mathrm{tr}(\Psi_{1}^{ii}(B)).

Using
Lemma
3
we see that

\displaystyle\lVert{\Psi^{ij}_{0}(B)}\rVert_{F}^{q}=O\left({\sigma_{i}^{q}\sigma_{j}^{q}}\right)\lVert{B}\rVert_{F}^{q}=(T-\tau)^{q/2}O\left({\sigma_{i}^{q}\sigma_{j}^{q}}\right)\lVert{B}\rVert^{q}=T^{q/2}O\left({\sigma_{i}^{q}\sigma_{j}^{q}}\right)\lVert{B}\rVert^{q},

and

{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T]}^{(L)\top}B{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[1:T]}^{(L)}]=1_{i=j}O(\sigma_{i}^{2}(T-\tau))\lVert{B}\rVert=1_{i=j}O(\sigma_{i}^{2}T)\lVert{B}\rVert,

both of which are uniform in
L
.

Since
(\phi_{il})_{l}
is summable and
(z_{it})
have uniformly bounded
4
-th moments, it is clear that
{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{(L)}/\sigma_{i}
converges to
{\mathbf{x}}_{i}/\sigma_{i}
in
L^{4}
as
L\to\infty
, for each fixed
T
. By the dominated convergence theorem with (
C.5
) as an upper-bound, we can take the limit as
L\to\infty
inside the expectation in (
C.5
) and the claim follows.

b
) follows from similar computations as in (a) and is omitted.

c
)
Note that
E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}E^{\top}E
has bounded operator norm under the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
defined in (
2.18
).
Since
a\asymp\sigma_{n}^{4}\gamma_{n}(\tau)^{2}
diverges as
T\to\infty
, the resolvent
R(a)
is well-defined under
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
and
\lVert{R(a)^{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}}\rVert=O(1)
.
After conditioning on the
\sigma
-algebra
{\mathcal{F}}
defined in (
2.22
), we can then apply (
a
) of the Lemma and get

\displaystyle\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}\left|{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{\top}R(a)^{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{\top}R(a)^{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}\right]\right|^{q}\lesssim T^{q/2}O(\sigma_{i}^{q}\sigma_{j}^{q}).

Taking expectations again to remove the conditioning, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left|{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{\top}R(a)^{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}\left[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{\top}R(a)^{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}\right]\right|^{q}\lesssim T^{q/2}O(\sigma_{i}^{q}\sigma_{j}^{q}).

Note that
\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{\top}R(a)^{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]=0
for all
i\neq j
by (
a
) of the Lemma. So

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}R(a)^{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}=1_{i=j}\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}R(a)^{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[\tau+1:T]}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]+O_{L^{q}}(\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}\sqrt{T}).

By Lemma
1
we have
1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}=1-o_{p}(1)
, from which the last claim follows.

d
) follows from similar computations to the above and is omitted.
∎

Note that the expectations appearing in the previous lemma are conditional on the noise series
\bm{\epsilon}
. The following lemma gives a preliminary computation on the unconditional moments of certain quadratic forms.
Recall matrices
B(a)
,
A(a)
and
Q(a)
:

\displaystyle A(a):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}

\displaystyle{X_{0}R(a)X_{\tau}^{\top}},\quad B(a):=\frac{1}{a}{X_{\tau}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R(a)X_{\tau}^{\top}},

\displaystyle Q(a):=I_{K}-a^{-1}X_{0}R_{a}E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}.

Lemma 6
.

Under the same setting as (
c
) of Lemma
5
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[

\displaystyle A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]=1_{i=j}\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}\gamma_{i}(\tau)}{a^{1/2}}+o(1)\right)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}(A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}})=O\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}\sigma_{j}^{2}}{aT}\right),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[B(a)_{ij}

\displaystyle 1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]=1_{i=j}o(1),\quad{\mathbb{E}}[Q(a)^{-1}_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]=1_{i=j}+o(1).

Proof.

Since
{\mathbf{x}}_{i}=\sigma_{i}{\mathbf{f}}_{i}+\bm{\epsilon}_{i}
, we first observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}T}{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}T}{\mathbb{E}}[\sigma_{i}^{2}{\mathbf{f}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}{\mathbf{f}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}]=1_{i=j}\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}\gamma_{i}(\tau)}{\sqrt{a}}.

(C.6)

By definition, the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
is independent from the vector
{\mathbf{x}}
. Therefore

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}T}{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]+\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}T}{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}(R(a)-I)1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}]

\displaystyle=1_{i=j}\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}\gamma_{i}(\tau)}{\sqrt{a}}{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{B}}_{0})+\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}T}{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}(R(a)-I)1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}]

\displaystyle=1_{i=j}\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}\gamma_{i}(\tau)}{\sqrt{a}}+o(1)\right)+\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}T}{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}(R(a)-I)1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}],

where the last equality follows since
{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{B}}_{0})=1+o(1)
by Lemma
1
.
It remains to compute the last expectation above.
Recall from (
2.12
) that the resolvent
R(a)
satisfies
R(a)-I=a^{-1}E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}E^{\top}ER(a)
. By definition of
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
we have
\lVert{E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}E^{\top}E1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}}\rVert=O(1)
and
\lVert{R(a)1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}}\rVert=O(1)
. Therefore

\displaystyle(R(a)-I)1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}=O_{\lVert{\cdot}\rVert}(a^{-1}).

(C.7)

Using (
C.7
) and (
a
) of Lemma
5
and taking iterated expectations we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}T}{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}(R(a)

\displaystyle-I)1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}T}{\mathbb{E}}\big[\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1,T-\tau]}^{\top}(R(a)-I)1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}{\mathbf{x}}_{j,[\tau+1:T]}]\big]

\displaystyle=1_{i=j}\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}T}O({\sigma_{i}^{2}}T){\mathbb{E}}[\lVert{R(a)-I}\rVert 1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]=1_{i=j}o(1).

For the second moment,
using
(a-b)^{2}=(a-c)^{2}+(c-b)^{2}+2(a-c)(c-b)
, we write

\displaystyle(A(a)_{ij}

\displaystyle 1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-{\mathbb{E}}[A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}])^{2}

(C.8)

\displaystyle=(A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}])^{2}+(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}])^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+2(A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}])(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[A_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]).

where by (
c
) of
Lemma
5
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[(A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}])^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{aT^{2}}O\left({\sigma_{i}^{2}\sigma_{j}^{2}T}\right)=O\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}\sigma_{j}^{2}}{aT}\right),

and from Lemma
9
(whose proof does not depend on the current lemma) we recall

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}])^{2}\right]=O\left(\frac{1}{aT}\right).

Taking expectation of (
C.8
) and using the Cauchy Schwarz inequality we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[(A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}[A(a)_{ij}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}])^{2}]=O\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}\sigma_{j}^{2}}{aT}\right).

The expectation of
B(a)
can be computed based on the same ideas and is omitted.

Lastly, under the event
{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}
, the matrix
Q(a)
is invertible with
\lVert{Q(a)1_{{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}}}\rVert=O(1)
. We recall from (
2.17
) that the inverse of
Q(a)
satisfies

\displaystyle Q(a)^{-1}=I_{K}+\frac{1}{a}Q(a)^{-1}X_{0}R(a)E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}.

By definition of
{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}
we know
1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}Q(a)^{-1}X_{0}R(a)E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}=O_{\lVert{\cdot}\rVert}(\sigma_{1}^{2})
and therefore

\displaystyle Q(a)^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}=1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}I_{K}+o_{\lVert{\cdot}\rVert}(1)

and the last claim follows after taking expectations.
∎

Appendix D
Estimates on resolvents

Define the following families of
\sigma
-algebras
({\mathcal{F}}_{i})_{i=1}^{p}
and
(\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}_{i})_{i=1}^{K}
by

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{i}:=\sigma\big(\bm{\epsilon}_{[K+1,K+i],[1:T]}\big),\quad\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}_{i}:=\sigma\big({\mathbf{x}}_{[1:i],[1:T]},\bm{\epsilon}_{[K+1,K+p],[1:T]}\big),

i.e.
{\mathcal{F}}_{i}
is the
\sigma
-algebra generated by first
i
coordinates of the noise series
\bm{\epsilon}
and
\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}_{i}
is generated by all
p
coordinates of
\bm{\epsilon}
plus the first
i
coordinates of the series
{\mathbf{x}}
.

Throughout the appendix we will write

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{i}[\ \cdot\ ]:={\mathbb{E}}_{i}[\ \cdot\ |{\mathcal{F}}_{i}],\quad\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i}[\ \cdot\ ]:={\mathbb{E}}_{i}[\ \cdot\ |\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}_{i}].

(D.1)

Note that by definition
{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[\ \cdot\ ]={\mathbb{E}}[\ \cdot\ ]
and

{\mathbb{E}}_{p}[\ \cdot\ ]=\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{0}[\ \cdot\ ]=\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[\ \cdot\ ]
.

We first develope a concentration inequality for normalized traces of the resolvent
R
.

Lemma 7
.

For any matrix
B
with
T-\tau
columns, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{tr}(B(R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-{\mathbb{E}}[R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]))

\displaystyle=O_{L^{2}}\left(\frac{\lVert{B}\rVert}{\theta\sqrt{T}}\right),

(a)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{tr}(B(E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-{\mathbb{E}}[E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}

\displaystyle R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]))=O_{L^{2}}\left(\frac{\lVert{B}\rVert}{\sqrt{T}}\right).

(b)

Proof.

a
)
Similar to Lemma
8
the proof is based on a martingale difference decomposition of
R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-{\mathbb{E}}[R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]
. We first setup the necessary notations and carry out some preliminary computations.

Recall that the
k
-th row of
E_{0}
is equal to
T^{-1/2}\bm{\epsilon}_{K+k,[1:T-\tau]}^{\top}
. For brevity of notation we will adopt the following notation

\displaystyle\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}:=\bm{\epsilon}_{K+k,[1:T-\tau]},\quad\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}:=\bm{\epsilon}_{K+k,[\tau+1:T]}.

(D.2)

Let
E_{k0}
and
E_{k\tau}
be the matrices
E_{0}
and
E_{\tau}
with the
k
-th row replaced by zeros, i.e.
E_{k0}:=E_{0}-T^{-1/2}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}
and

E_{k\tau}:=E_{0}-T^{-1/2}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}
. Define

\displaystyle\underline{R}_{k}:=\Big(I_{T}-\frac{1}{\theta}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}\Big)^{-1},\quad R_{k}:=\Big(I_{T}-\frac{1}{\theta}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0}\Big)^{-1},

where
R_{k}
is not to be confused with
R_{\theta}
and
R_{\lambda}
defined previously.
Then

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}-E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0}=\frac{1}{T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top},\quad E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}-E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}=\frac{1}{T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top},

from which we can compute

\displaystyle R^{-1}-\underline{R}_{k}^{-1}=-\frac{1}{\theta}(E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}-E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau})E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}=-\frac{1}{\theta T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}

\displaystyle\underline{R}_{k}^{-1}-R_{k}^{-1}=-\frac{1}{\theta}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}(E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}-E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0})=-\frac{1}{\theta T}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}.

We furthermore define scalars

\displaystyle\underline{\beta}_{k}=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{tr}(\underline{R}_{k}(R^{-1}-\underline{R}_{k}^{-1}))}=\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{\theta T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}\underline{R}_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}},

\displaystyle\beta_{k}=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{tr}(R_{k}(\underline{R}_{k}^{-1}-R_{k}^{-1}))}=\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{\theta T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}},

both of which are clearly of order
1+o(1)
under the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
.
Using (
C.1
) we get

\displaystyle R-\underline{R}_{k}=-\underline{\beta}_{k}\underline{R}_{k}(R^{-1}-\underline{R}_{k}^{-1})\underline{R}_{k}=\frac{\underline{\beta}_{k}}{\theta T}\underline{R}_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}\underline{R}_{k},

(D.3a)

\displaystyle\underline{R}_{k}-R_{k}=-\beta_{k}R_{k}(\underline{R}_{k}^{-1}-R_{k}^{-1})R_{k}=\frac{\beta_{k}}{\theta T}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}.

(D.3b)

Substituting (
D.3b
) back into (
D.3a
) we get

\displaystyle R-\underline{R}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\underline{\beta}_{k}}{\theta T}\Big(R_{k}+\frac{\beta_{k}}{\theta T}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}\Big)\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}\Big(R_{k}+\frac{\beta_{k}}{\theta T}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}\Big),

and so we have

\displaystyle R-R_{k}=(\underline{R}_{k}-R_{k})+(R-\underline{R}_{k})

\displaystyle=:U_{1}+U_{2}+U_{3}+U_{4}+U_{5},

(D.4)

where we have defined

\displaystyle U_{1}:=\frac{\beta_{k}}{\theta T}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k},\quad U_{2}:=\frac{\underline{\beta}_{k}}{\theta T}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R_{k},

(D.5)

\displaystyle U_{3}:=\frac{\underline{\beta}_{k}\beta_{k}}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k},

\displaystyle U_{4}:=\frac{\underline{\beta}_{k}\beta_{k}}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R_{k},

\displaystyle U_{5}:=\frac{\underline{\beta}_{k}\beta_{k}^{2}}{\theta^{3}T^{3}}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}.

Recall the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
from (
1
). Define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}:=\left\{\lVert{E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0}}\rVert+\lVert{E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}}\rVert\leq 4\left(1+\frac{p}{T}\right)\right\},\quad k=1,\ldots,p.

(D.6)

Clearly
\lVert{E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0}}\rVert\leq\lVert{E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}}\rVert
which implies
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}
and so
1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}\leq 1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}
.
Recall the family of conditional expectations
{\mathbb{E}}_{i}[\ \cdot\ ]
defined in (
D.1
). Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{tr}(B

\displaystyle(R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-{\mathbb{E}}[R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]))=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{p}({\mathbb{E}}_{k}-{\mathbb{E}}_{k-1})\mathrm{tr}(BR1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{p}({\mathbb{E}}_{k}-{\mathbb{E}}_{k-1})\left(\mathrm{tr}(BR1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}})-\mathrm{tr}(BR_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{p}({\mathbb{E}}_{k}-{\mathbb{E}}_{k-1})\mathrm{tr}(B(R-R_{k})1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}})-\frac{1}{T}\sum_{k=1}^{p}({\mathbb{E}}_{k}-{\mathbb{E}}_{k-1})\mathrm{tr}(BR_{k}(1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}-1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}))

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2},

(D.7)

where the second equality holds
since
{\mathbb{E}}_{k}[\mathrm{tr}(BR_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}})]={\mathbb{E}}_{k-1}[\mathrm{tr}(BR_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}})]
and the third equality is purely algebraic computations. We first deal with the second term in (
D.7
). Using
\mathrm{tr}(BR_{k})\leq p\lVert{BR_{k}}\rVert
and
\lVert{BR_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}}\rVert=O(\lVert{B}\rVert)
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|I_{2}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\mathbb{E}}\left|({\mathbb{E}}_{k}-{\mathbb{E}}_{k-1})\mathrm{tr}(BR_{k}(1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}-1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}))\right|^{2}\leq\frac{4p^{2}}{T^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\mathbb{E}}\left|\lVert{BR_{k}}\rVert(1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}-1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{p^{2}}{T^{2}}\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}\right)\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\mathbb{E}}\left|1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}-1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}\right|^{2}=O\left(\frac{p^{2}}{T^{2}}\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}\right)\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\mathbb{P}}({\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{c})=o(T^{-l}\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}),

for any
l\in\mathbb{N}
by Lemma
1
.
For the first term in (
D.7
), since
I_{1}
is a sum of a martingale difference sequence, using (
D.4
) and
{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|I_{1}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\mathbb{E}}\big|({\mathbb{E}}_{k}-{\mathbb{E}}_{k-1})\mathrm{tr}(B(R-R_{k})1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}})\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{T^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\mathbb{E}}\big|\mathrm{tr}(B(R-R_{k})1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}})\big|^{2}\leq\frac{20}{T^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}\sum_{n=1}^{5}{\mathbb{E}}\big|\mathrm{tr}(BU_{n}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}})\big|^{2},

and it remains to bound the second moment of each
\mathrm{tr}(BU_{n}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}})
.
Since
\{\epsilon_{it}\}
are assumed to be i.i.d. standard Gaussian, we have the following moment estimate

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lVert{\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}}\rVert^{n}\right]={\mathbb{E}}\Bigg[\Big(\sum_{t=1}^{T-\tau}\epsilon_{kt}^{2}\Big)^{n/2}\Bigg]\lesssim(T-\tau)^{n/2-1}\sum_{t=1}^{T-\tau}{\mathbb{E}}|\epsilon_{kt}|^{n}=O(T^{n/2}).

(D.8)

Using
\beta_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}=1+o(1)
and the trivial inequality
x^{\top}Ax\leq\lVert{x}\rVert^{2}\lVert{A}\rVert
we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}

\displaystyle\big|\mathrm{tr}(BU_{1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}})\big|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}BR_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0})^{2}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\right]

(D.9a)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lVert{\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}}\rVert^{4}\lVert{R_{k}}\rVert^{4}\lVert{E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}}\rVert^{2}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\right]\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}.

The second term
U_{2}
can be dealt in exactly the same way to obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|\mathrm{tr}(BU_{2}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}})\big|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}\lVert{B}\rVert^{2},

(D.9b)

and we omit the details. For
U_{3}
, similar computations gives

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|\mathrm{tr}(BU_{3}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}})\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta^{4}T^{4}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[(\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}BR_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0})^{2}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\theta^{4}T^{4}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lVert{\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}}\rVert^{4}\lVert{\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}}\rVert^{4}\lVert{R_{k}}\rVert^{4}\lVert{E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}}\rVert^{2}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\right]\lVert{B}\rVert^{2},

since
x^{\top}Ay\leq\lVert{x}\rVert\lVert{y}\rVert\lVert{A}\rVert
. Therefore

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|\mathrm{tr}(BU_{3})\big|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta^{4}T^{4}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lVert{\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}}\rVert^{8}\right]^{1/2}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lVert{\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}}\rVert^{8}\right]^{1/2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta^{4}}\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}.

(D.9c)

Once again
U_{4}
can be bounded in the same way to obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|\mathrm{tr}(BU_{4})1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\big|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\theta^{4}}\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}.

(D.9d)

With the same approach but more laborious computations we can obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|\mathrm{tr}(BU_{5})1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\big|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta^{6}}\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}.

(D.9e)

Note that the estimates (
D.9a
)-(
D.9e
) are uniform in
k=1,\ldots,p
. We then conclude

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big|\frac{1}{T}

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(B(R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}-{\mathbb{E}}[R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}]))\Big|^{2}=O\left(\frac{p}{T^{2}\theta^{2}}\right)\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}=O\left(\frac{1}{T\theta^{2}}\right)\lVert{B}\rVert^{2},

and the conclusion follows.

b
)
Similar to (
a
), via a martingale difference decomposition we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big|\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{tr}

\displaystyle(B(E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}-{\mathbb{E}}[E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}]))\Big|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{T^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\mathbb{E}}\big|\mathrm{tr}(B(E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R-E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0}R_{k}))1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\big|^{2},

where, recalling the
U_{n}
’s defined in the proof of (
a
), we have

\displaystyle E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R-E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0}R_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}+\frac{1}{T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}(R-R_{k})+E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0}(R-R_{k})

(D.10)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{n=1}^{5}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}U_{n}+\sum_{n=1}^{5}E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0}U_{n}.

We deal with the first two term in (
D.10
) to illustrate the ideas of the proof, the other terms can be dealth with similarly. Using (
D.8
) and
p\asymp T
, clearly we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\mathbb{E}}\Big|\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{tr}(B\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k})1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\Big|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{T^{4}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}T^{2}{\mathbb{E}}[\lVert{BR_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}}\rVert^{2}]=O\left(\frac{1}{T}\right)\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}.

(D.11)

Similar to the computations in (
D.9a
), we can get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big|\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{tr}(B(

\displaystyle\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}U_{1}))1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\Big|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}\frac{1}{T^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}[(\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}B\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0})^{2}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T^{4}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[\lVert{\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}}\rVert^{8}\lVert{R_{k}}\rVert^{4}\lVert{E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}}\rVert^{2}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\right]\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}\lVert{B}\rVert^{2},

which immediately gives

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\mathbb{E}}\Big|\frac{1}{T}\mathrm{tr}(B(

\displaystyle\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}U_{1}))1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\Big|^{2}=O\left(\frac{p}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}\right)\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}=O\left(\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T}\right)\lVert{B}\rVert^{2}.

Note that this term is negligible in comparison to (
D.11
).
Using the same ideas,
it is routine to check that the other 9 terms in (
D.10
) are negligible as well, and we omit the details. The bound therefore follows from (
D.11
).
∎

Next recall that

Q=I_{K}-\frac{1}{\theta}X_{0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}.

We now state a concentration inequality for entries of the matrix
Q^{-1}
, under the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
.

Lemma 8
.

Write
Q_{ij}^{-1}:=(Q^{-1})_{ij}
. Then

a)

For all
k=1,\ldots,K
, we have

\displaystyle Q_{kk}^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[Q_{kk}^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]=O_{L^{1}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{k}^{2}}{\theta\sqrt{T}}\right).

b)

The off-diagonal elements of
Q^{-1}
satisfies

\displaystyle Q_{ij}^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}=O_{L^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}\sigma_{j}}{\theta\sqrt{T}}\right)

uniformly in
i,j=1,\ldots,K
,
i\neq j
.

Proof.

a
)
Recalling the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
, we note that the matrix
Q
is invertible with probability tending to 1.
The proof relies on expressing
Q_{kk}^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[Q_{kk}^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]
as a sum of martingale differences. We first setup the notations necessary.

Let
T^{-1/2}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}:=T^{-1/2}{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}
be the (column vector) of the
i
-th row of
X_{0}
, i.e. we can write
X_{0}=T^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{K}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}
. Define

X_{i0}:=X_{0}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top},

and

\displaystyle Q_{(i)}:=I_{K}-\frac{1}{\theta}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top},\quad Q_{(ii)}:=I_{K}-\frac{1}{\theta}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{i0}^{\top},

from which we can immediately compute

\displaystyle Q-Q_{(i)}=-\frac{1}{\theta\sqrt{T}}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top},\quad Q_{(i)}-Q_{(ii)}=-\frac{1}{\theta\sqrt{T}}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}.

Note that all elements on the
i
-th row of
Q_{(i)}
are equal to zero except for the diagonal which is equal to 1, i.e.
Q_{(i)}
is equal to the identity when restricted to the
i
-th coordinate. Then the inverse
Q_{(i)}^{-1}
, whenever it exists, must also equal to the identity when restricted to the
i
-th coordinate. A similar observation can be made for the matrix
Q_{(ii)}
and it is not hard to observe that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}

\displaystyle(Q_{(ii)})^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}=1,\quad{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}(Q_{(i)})^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{j}=0,\quad\forall j\neq i,

(D.12)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}(Q_{(ii)})^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{j}={\mathbf{e}}_{j}^{\top}(Q_{(ii)})^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}=0,\quad\forall j\neq i.

To compute the difference
Q^{-1}-Q_{(i)}^{-1}
, which will turn out to be the central focus of the proof, we first define the following scalars

\displaystyle b_{i}:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{tr}(Q_{(i)}^{-1}(Q-Q_{(i)}))}=\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{\theta\sqrt{T}}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(i)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}}

(D.13)

\displaystyle b_{ii}:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{tr}(Q_{(ii)}^{-1}(Q_{(i)}-Q_{(ii)}))}=\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{\theta\sqrt{T}}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}}=1,

where the last equality holds by (
D.12
). Then using the identity (
C.1
) we have

\displaystyle Q^{-1}-Q_{(i)}^{-1}=\frac{b_{i}}{\theta\sqrt{T}}Q_{(i)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(i)}^{-1},

(D.14a)

\displaystyle Q_{(i)}^{-1}-Q_{(ii)}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\theta\sqrt{T}}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}.

(D.14b)

We observe that the matrices
Q_{(i)}^{-1}
and
Q_{(ii)}^{-1}
differ only on off-diagonal elements on the
i
-th column. Indeed, from
(
D.12
) and (
D.14b
), if
n\neq i
or if
n=m=i
then

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{m}^{\top}(Q_{(i)}^{-1}-Q_{(ii)}^{-1}){\mathbf{e}}_{n}=\frac{1}{\theta\sqrt{T}}{\mathbf{e}}_{m}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{n}=0.

(D.15)

Then, substituting (
D.14b
) back into (
D.14a
) we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}(Q^{-1}-

\displaystyle Q_{(i)}^{-1}){\mathbf{e}}_{k}=\frac{b_{i}}{\theta\sqrt{T}}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}Q_{(i)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(i)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{i}}{\theta\sqrt{T}}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}Q_{(i)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+\frac{b_{i}}{\theta^{2}T}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}Q_{(i)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{b_{i}}{\theta\sqrt{T}}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+\frac{b_{i}}{\theta^{2}T}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}Q_{(i)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad+\frac{b_{i}}{\theta^{2}T}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}

\displaystyle=:I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}.

(D.16)

To simplify this expression further, define the following quadratic forms

\displaystyle\xi_{i}:=\frac{1}{\theta T}

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\quad\eta_{i}:=\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{i0}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{i},

\displaystyle\zeta_{ik}:=\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{i0}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{i},

(D.17)

then using (
D.12
), we can easily write
I_{1},I_{2}
and
I_{3}
into

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=1_{i=k}\frac{b_{k}}{\theta\sqrt{T}}{\mathbf{x}}_{k}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(kk)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}=1_{i=k}b_{k}\xi_{k},

(D.18)

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=1_{i=k}\frac{b_{k}}{\theta^{2}T}{\mathbf{x}}_{k}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{k0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{k}=1_{i=k}b_{k}\eta_{k},

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=1_{i\neq k}\frac{b_{i}}{\theta^{2}T}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}=1_{i\neq k}b_{i}\zeta_{ik}.

We first state some estimates on
\xi
and
\eta
under the appropriate events. Recall from (
2.18
) the event

{\mathcal{B}}_{1}:=\left\{\lVert{X_{0}^{\top}X_{0}}\rVert\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{K}\sigma_{i}^{2}\right\}
. Define the event

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{1}^{i}:=\Big\{\lVert{X_{i0}^{\top}X_{i0}}\rVert\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{K}\sigma_{i}^{2}\Big\},\quad i=1,\ldots,K

(D.19)

and write
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{i}:={\mathcal{B}}_{0}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{1}^{i}
. Then clearly
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{i}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
.
Define

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}_{i}:=\frac{1}{\theta T}\mathrm{tr}\big(\Psi_{0}^{ii}(RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau})\big),\quad\overline{\eta}_{i}:=\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T}\mathrm{tr}\big(\Psi^{ii}_{0}(RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{i0}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau})\big),

where
\Psi^{ii}_{0}
is defined in (
C.4
). Write

\displaystyle\underline{\xi}_{i}:=\xi_{i}-\overline{\xi}_{i},\quad\underline{\eta}_{i}:=\eta_{i}-\overline{\eta}_{i}.

(D.20)

Using Lemma
5
and taking iterated expectations we have

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt{\mathbb{E}}\big[\underline{\xi}_{i}^{2}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{i}}\big]={\mathbb{E}}\big[\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[\underline{\xi}_{i}^{2}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{i}}]\big]=\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}O(\sigma_{i}^{4}T){\mathbb{E}}\lVert{RE_{\tau}^{\top}E1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{i}}}\rVert^{2}=O\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{4}}{\theta^{2}T}\right),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big[\underline{\eta}_{i}^{2}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{i}}\big]=\frac{1}{\theta^{4}T^{2}}O(\sigma_{i}^{4}T){\mathbb{E}}\lVert{RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{i0}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{i}}}\rVert^{2}=O\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{4}\sum_{j=1}^{K}\sigma_{j}^{4}}{\theta^{4}T}\right).

By Lemma
3
we also have

\displaystyle\overline{\xi}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}^{i}_{2}}=O(\sigma_{i}^{2}\theta^{-1})

\displaystyle,\quad\overline{\eta}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}^{i}_{2}}=O\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{K}\sigma_{j}^{2}}{\theta^{2}}\right).

(D.21)

We then consider the scalar
b_{i}
defined in (
D.13
). From (
D.12
) and (
D.14b
) we observe

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\theta\sqrt{T}}

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(i)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}=\frac{1}{\theta\sqrt{T}}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}X_{i0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}=\xi_{i}+\eta_{i}.

Substituting back into (
D.13
)
we can simplify to obtain

\displaystyle b_{i}=(1-\xi_{i}-\eta_{i})^{-1}.

(D.22)

Define

\overline{b}_{i}=(1-\overline{\xi}_{i}-\overline{\eta}_{i})^{-1}
so that subtracting the two we get

\displaystyle b_{i}=(1-\xi_{i}-\eta_{i})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\overline{b}_{i}-b_{i}\overline{b}_{i}(\underline{\xi}_{i}+\underline{\eta}_{i}).

(D.23)

Finally, from the expression (
D.22
) and the bounds (
D.21
) we clearly have

\displaystyle b_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}=1+o(1),\quad\overline{b}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{i}}=1+o(1).

(D.24)

We can now carry out the main idea of the proof. Recall notations
\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[\ \cdot\ ]
and

\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i}[\ \cdot\ ]
from (
D.1
). By definition of
Q_{(ii)}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{i}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}\big(Q^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[

\displaystyle Q^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]\big){\mathbf{e}}_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1})\left({\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}Q^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}-{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}Q_{(ii)}^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{i}}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1})\left({\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}Q^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}-{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}Q_{(i)}^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{i}}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}\right),

where the last equality follows from (
D.15
).
Similar to how we dealt with the second term in (
D.7
) in the proof of Lemma
7
, using Lemma
1
we may obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{k}^{\top}\big(Q^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}

\displaystyle[Q^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]\big){\mathbf{e}}_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1}){\mathbf{e}}_{k}\left(Q^{-1}-Q_{(ii)}^{-1}\right)1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}{\mathbf{e}}_{k}+O_{L^{2}}(KT^{-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1})(I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3})1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}+O_{L^{2}}(KT^{-1}),

(D.25)

where the
second equality holds by (
D.15
).

As will be shown, the term involving
I_{1}
is the leading term of (
D.25
), this is what we consider now.
Using the identity (
D.18
) we simply have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1})I_{1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{i}}=(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k-1})b_{k}\xi_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}},

which, recalling (
D.20
) and using (
D.23
), can be written into

\displaystyle(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k-1})

\displaystyle b_{k}\xi_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}=(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k-1})\left(\overline{b}_{k}-b_{k}\overline{b}_{k}(\underline{\xi}_{k}+\underline{\eta}_{k})\right)\left(\overline{\xi}_{k}+\underline{\xi}_{k}\right)1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}

\displaystyle=(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k-1})\Big[\overline{b}_{k}\overline{\xi}_{k}+\overline{b}_{k}\underline{\xi}_{k}-b_{k}\overline{b}_{k}(\underline{\xi}_{k}+\underline{\eta}_{k})(\overline{\xi}_{k}+\underline{\xi}_{k})\Big]1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}.

(D.26)

We consider the three terms in the square bracket in (
D.26
) separately. For the first term, we note that
(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k-1})\overline{b}_{k}\overline{\xi}_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}}=0
by definition of
\overline{b}_{k}\overline{\xi}_{k}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}
. Using this, we have

\displaystyle(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k-1})\overline{b}_{k}\overline{\xi}_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}=0-(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k-1})\overline{b}_{k}\overline{\xi}_{k}(1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}}-1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}).

Recalling (
D.21
) and (
D.24
) and using Assumptions
1
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k-1})\overline{b}_{k}\overline{\xi}_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big|

\displaystyle\leq 2{\mathbb{E}}\big|\overline{b}_{k}\overline{\xi}_{k}(1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}}-1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}})\big|=O(\sigma_{k}^{2}\theta^{-1}){\mathbb{E}}|1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}}-1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}|=O(\sigma_{k}^{2}\theta^{-1}KT^{-1}),

where the last equality follows from the fact that
{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}
and Lemma
1
. For the second term in (
D.26
), using
{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}
, (
D.24
) and (
D.21
) we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k-1})

\displaystyle\overline{b}_{k}\underline{\xi}_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big|^{2}\lesssim 4{\mathbb{E}}\big|\overline{b}_{k}\underline{\xi}_{k}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}}\big|^{2}=O\left(\frac{\sigma_{k}^{4}}{\theta^{2}T}\right).

Similarly the third term of (
D.26
) is bounded by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k}-

\displaystyle\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k-1})\big[b_{k}\overline{b}_{k}(\underline{\xi}_{k}+\underline{\eta}_{k})(\overline{\xi}_{k}+\underline{\xi}_{k})1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big]\big|\lesssim 2{\mathbb{E}}\big|(\underline{\xi}_{k}+\underline{\eta}_{k})(\overline{\xi}_{k}+\underline{\xi}_{k})1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}}\big|.

Expanding, applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and using (
D.24
) and (
D.21
), we may obtain a bound of order
o_{L^{1}}(T^{-1/2})
; we omit the repetitive details.
Substituting the above bounds back into equation (
D.26
) we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|\sum_{i=1}^{K}(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{i-1})I_{1}\big|={\mathbb{E}}\Big|(\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k}-\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}_{k-1})b_{k}\xi_{k}\Big|=O\left(\frac{\sigma_{k}^{2}}{\theta\sqrt{T}}\right).

The cases of
I_{2}
and
I_{3}
can be dealt with with similar approaches and we omit the details. In fact, from the definitions in (
D.18
) it is not difficult to see that
\eta
and
\zeta
are higher order terms relative to
\xi
under the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
.
It can therefore be shown that the term involving
I_{1}
is the leading term in (
D.25
)
and the claim follows.

b
)
Define
\overline{Q}:=I_{T}-\theta^{-1}X_{0}^{\top}X_{0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}
so that similar to (
2.17
) we have

\displaystyle Q^{-1}-I_{K}=\frac{1}{\theta}X_{0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}(I_{K}-\theta^{-1}X_{0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top})^{-1}=\frac{1}{\theta}X_{0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}\overline{Q}^{-1}X_{0}^{\top}.

Recall that we have
X_{0}^{\top}X_{0}=T^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{K}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}
, define the matrices

\displaystyle\overline{Q}_{(j)}:=I_{T}-\frac{1}{\theta T}\sum_{k\neq j}{\mathbf{x}}_{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{k}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau},\quad\overline{Q}_{(ij)}:=I_{T}-\frac{1}{\theta T}\sum_{k\neq i,j}{\mathbf{x}}_{k}{\mathbf{x}}_{k}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau},

so that

\overline{Q}-\overline{Q}_{(j)}=-\frac{1}{\theta T}{\mathbf{x}}_{j}{\mathbf{x}}_{j}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}
and

\overline{Q}_{(j)}-\overline{Q}_{(ij)}=-\frac{1}{\theta T}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}
. Let

\displaystyle a_{j}:=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{tr}(\overline{Q}_{(j)}^{-1}(\overline{Q}-\overline{Q}_{(j)}))}=\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{\theta T}{\mathbf{x}}_{j}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}\overline{Q}_{(j)}^{-1}{\mathbf{x}}_{j}},

\displaystyle a_{ij}:=\frac{1}{1+\mathrm{tr}(\overline{Q}_{(ij)}^{-1}(\overline{Q}_{(j)}-\overline{Q}_{(ij)}))}=\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{\theta T}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}\overline{Q}_{(ij)}^{-1}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}},

then by (
C.2
) we have

\displaystyle\overline{Q}^{-1}{\mathbf{x}}_{j}=a_{j}\overline{Q}_{(j)}^{-1}{\mathbf{x}}_{j},\quad{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}\overline{Q}_{(i)}^{-1}=a_{ij}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}\overline{Q}_{(ij)}^{-1}.

We can therefore write

\displaystyle Q^{-1}_{ij}=\frac{1}{\theta T}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}\overline{Q}^{-1}{\mathbf{x}}_{j}=\frac{a_{j}a_{ij}}{\theta T}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}\overline{Q}_{(ij)}^{-1}{\mathbf{x}}_{j}.

Now define
X_{ij0}:=X_{0}-T^{-1/2}({\mathbf{e}}_{i}{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}+{\mathbf{e}}_{j}{\mathbf{x}}_{j})
and
events

{\mathcal{B}}_{1}^{ij}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{ij}
analogous to
(
D.19
) with
X_{i0}
replaced by
X_{ij0}
. Similar to (a) of the Lemma we have

a_{j}=1+o(1)
and
a_{ij}=1+o(1)
under the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
. Therefore we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|Q_{ij}^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\big|{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}\overline{Q}_{(ij)}^{-1}{\mathbf{x}}_{j}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}{\mathbb{E}}\big|{\mathbf{x}}_{i}^{\top}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}\overline{Q}_{(ij)}^{-1}{\mathbf{x}}_{j}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{ij}}\big|^{2}.

By Lemma
5
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|Q_{ij}^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big|^{2}=\frac{1}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}O(\sigma_{i}^{2}\sigma_{j}^{2}T),

which completes the whole proof.
∎

We finally show that the conditional expectations of diagonal elements of
A
,
B
and
Q^{-1}
, defined in (
2.15
), are sufficiently close to the unconditional expectation.

Lemma 9
.

For each
i=1,\ldots,K
, we have

\displaystyle\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[A_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[A_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]=O_{L^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{{\theta^{3/2}\sqrt{T}}}\right),\quad\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[

\displaystyle B_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[B_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]=O_{L^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{{\theta\sqrt{T}}}\right),

\displaystyle\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[Q^{-1}_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[Q^{-1}_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]

\displaystyle=O_{L^{2}}\left(\frac{K\lVert{{\bm{\sigma}}}\rVert_{\ell_{2}}^{2}}{\theta\sqrt{T}}\right).

Proof.

From (a) of Lemma
5
we recall that

\displaystyle\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[A_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}T}\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[1:T-\tau]}^{\top}R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}{\mathbf{x}}_{i,[\tau+1:T]}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}T}\mathrm{tr}(\Psi^{ii}_{1}(R))1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}

(D.27)

where, using (
C.4
) and the cyclic property of the trace, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(\Psi^{ii}_{1}(R))=\mathrm{tr}\left(({\mathbf{0}},I_{T-\tau})(\sigma_{i}^{2}\Phi_{i}^{\top}\Phi_{i}+I_{T})(I_{T-\tau},{\mathbf{0}})^{\top}R\right)=:\mathrm{tr}(GR).

(D.28)

Furthermore, using (a) of Lemma
3
, we see that

\displaystyle G:=({\mathbf{0}},I_{T-\tau})(\sigma_{i}^{2}\Phi_{i}^{\top}\Phi_{i}+I_{T})(I_{T-\tau},{\mathbf{0}})^{\top}=O_{\lVert{\cdot}\rVert}(\sigma_{i}^{2}).

(D.29)

From (
D.27
) we have
{\mathbb{E}}[A_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]={\mathbb{E}}[\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[A_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}T}{\mathbb{E}}[\mathrm{tr}(\Psi^{ii}_{1}(R))1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]
and so

\displaystyle\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[A_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]

\displaystyle-{\mathbb{E}}[A_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}T}\left(\mathrm{tr}(\Psi^{ii}_{1}(R))1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-{\mathbb{E}}[\mathrm{tr}(\Psi^{ii}_{1}(R))1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}T}\left(\mathrm{tr}(GR)1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-{\mathbb{E}}[\mathrm{tr}(GR)1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}T}\mathrm{tr}\big(G(R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-{\mathbb{E}}[R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}])\big),

by linearity of the expectation and the trace.
By (
a
) of Lemma
7
we have

\displaystyle\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[A_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[A_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\theta}}O_{L^{2}}\left(\frac{\lVert{G}\rVert}{\theta\sqrt{T}}\right)=O_{L^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{{\theta^{3/2}\sqrt{T}}}\right),

where the last equality follows from (
D.29
).
For the case of
B
, similar computations and (
b
) of Lemma
7
give

\displaystyle\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[B_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]

\displaystyle-{\mathbb{E}}[B_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]=\frac{1}{\theta T}\left(\mathrm{tr}(\Psi^{ii}_{1}(E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R))1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-{\mathbb{E}}[\mathrm{tr}(\Psi^{ii}_{1}(E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R))1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta T}\mathrm{tr}\left(G(E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}-{\mathbb{E}}[E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}}]\right)=\frac{1}{\theta}O_{L^{2}}\left(\frac{\lVert{G}\rVert}{\sqrt{T}}\right)=O_{L^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{{\theta\sqrt{T}}}\right).

Finally we consider
\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[Q_{ii}^{-1}]
. We recall from (
2.17
) that

\displaystyle Q:=I_{K}-\frac{1}{\theta}X_{0}RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}X_{0}^{\top}.

(D.30)

The strategy of the proof, similar to that of Lemma
7
and Lemma
8
, is express
\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[Q^{-1}_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[Q^{-1}_{ii}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]
into a sum of martingale difference sequence. We first introduce the necessary notations and carry out some algebraic computations.

Similar to (
D.2
),
we will define
\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}:=\bm{\epsilon}_{K+k,[1:T-\tau]}
and
\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}:=\bm{\epsilon}_{K+k,[\tau+1:T]}
.
Recall from (
D.4
) that
R-R_{k}=\sum_{n=1}^{5}U_{n}
, where
the
U_{n}
’s are defined in (
D.5
).
Similar to the computations in (
D.10
), we may obtain

\displaystyle RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}

\displaystyle-R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}=\frac{1}{T}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}+\sum_{n=1}^{5}U_{n}E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}=:V+W,

where we defined

\displaystyle V:=\frac{1}{T}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}+(U_{2}+U_{3})E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau},

\displaystyle W:=(U_{1}+U_{4}+U_{5})E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}.

Define matrices
V_{1},V_{2},V_{3},W_{1},W_{2},W_{3}
by,

\displaystyle V_{1}:=I_{T},\quad

\displaystyle V_{2}:=\frac{\underline{\beta}_{k}}{\theta}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R_{k}E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau},\quad V_{3}:=\frac{\underline{\beta}_{k}\beta_{k}}{\theta^{2}T}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\bm{\epsilon}_{k0}\bm{\epsilon}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}

\displaystyle W_{1}:=\beta_{k}R_{k}E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau},\quad W_{2}:=\frac{\underline{\beta}_{k}\beta_{k}}{\theta T}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R_{k}E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau},

\displaystyle W_{3}:=\frac{\underline{\beta}_{k}\beta_{k}^{2}}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau},

so that using (
D.5
) we can decompose
V
and
W
into

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}(V_{1}+V_{2}+V_{3})

(D.31a)

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\theta T}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}(W_{1}+W_{2}+W_{3}).

(D.31b)

It is clear that
V
and
W
are matrices of rank one.
We define

\displaystyle\underline{Q}_{(k)}:

\displaystyle=I_{K}-\frac{1}{\theta}X_{0}(RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}-V)X_{0}^{\top},

\displaystyle\underline{Q}_{(kk)}:

\displaystyle=I_{K}-\frac{1}{\theta}X_{0}(RE_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}-V-W)X_{0}^{\top},

then from (
D.30
) we can write
Q-\underline{Q}_{(k)}=-\theta^{-1}X_{0}VX_{0}^{\top}
and

Q_{(k)}-\underline{Q}_{(kk)}=-\theta^{-1}X_{0}WX_{0}^{\top}
.
Define the following scalars quantities

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}:=\frac{1}{1-\theta^{-1}\mathrm{tr}(Q_{(k)}^{-1}X_{0}VX_{0}^{\top})},\quad\alpha_{kk}:=\frac{1}{1-\theta^{-1}\mathrm{tr}(Q_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}WX_{0}^{\top})},

then using (
C.1
) we obtain

\displaystyle Q^{-1}=\underline{Q}_{(k)}^{-1}+\frac{1}{\theta}\underline{Q}_{(k)}^{-1}X_{0}VX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(k)}^{-1},\quad\underline{Q}_{(k)}^{-1}=\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}+\frac{1}{\theta}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}WX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}.

Substituting the second identity into the first gives

\displaystyle Q^{-1}-\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\theta}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}WX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\theta}\Big(\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}+\frac{1}{\theta}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}WX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}\Big)X_{0}VX_{0}^{\top}\Big(\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}+\frac{1}{\theta}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}WX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}\Big),

which after simplifying becomes

\displaystyle Q^{-1}-\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\theta}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}VX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}+\frac{1}{\theta}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}WX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}

(D.32)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}VX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}WX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}+\frac{1}{\theta^{2}}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}WX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}VX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}.

Before we proceed with the proof we first prove some moment estimates for the terms in (
D.32
).
We start with some informal observations. By comparing (
D.31a
) and (
D.31a
), we see that the matrix
W
is smaller in magnitude in comparison to
V
by a factor of
\theta^{-1}
. This suggests that the first term in (
D.32
) is the leading term while the rest are high order terms in comparison and we will therefore only deal with first term in detail below. The same arguments can be applied to the rest of (
D.32
) to make the above argument rigorous, but we omit the repetitive details.

Recall the family of event
\{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k},k=1,\ldots,p\}
from (
D.6
) and define
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}:={\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
.
From definition we note that
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}
.
Furthermore, from Lemma
1
we have

\displaystyle 1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}}-1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\leq 1-1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}=o_{p}(T^{-l}),\quad\forall l\in\mathbb{N}.

(D.33)

In the computations below, we will often substitute
1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}}
with
1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}
and vice versa in expectations. Whenever we do so, we may use (
D.33
) and a similar argument to how we dealt with (
D.7
) to show that the error term of such a substitution is negligible for the purpose of the proof.
Hence from now on we will use the two indicators
1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}^{k}}
and
1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}
interchangeably below without further justifications.

Since we can write
X_{0}^{\top}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{l=1}^{K}{\mathbf{x}}_{l}{\mathbf{e}}_{l}^{\top}
, the first term in (
D.32
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\theta}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}

\displaystyle\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}VX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}=\frac{1}{\theta T}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{K}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{l}({\mathbf{x}}_{l}^{\top}V{\mathbf{x}}_{m}){\mathbf{e}}_{m}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i},

(D.34)

where, recalling (
D.31a
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{x}}_{l}^{\top}V{\mathbf{x}}_{m}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{n=1}^{3}{\mathbf{x}}_{l}^{\top}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}V_{n}{\mathbf{x}}_{m}.

(D.35)

Using (
D.34
)-(
D.35
) and the inequality

(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i})^{p}\lesssim n^{p-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{p}
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big|\frac{1}{\theta}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}

\displaystyle VX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\Big|^{2}\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{K}{\mathbb{E}}\Big|{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{l}({\mathbf{x}}_{l}^{\top}V{\mathbf{x}}_{m}){\mathbf{e}}_{m}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{3K^{2}}{\theta^{2}T^{4}}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{K}\sum_{n=1}^{3}{\mathbb{E}}\Big|{\mathbf{x}}_{l}^{\top}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}V_{n}{\mathbf{x}}_{m}\lVert{\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}}\rVert^{2}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\Big|^{2}.

(D.36)

Note that under the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
, we can easily see that
\lVert{Q^{-1}_{(kk)}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}}\rVert=O(1)
. Therefore by the Cauchy Schwarz inequality we can obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{\eqref{equation - QXVXQ}}\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\theta^{2}T^{4}}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{K}\sum_{n=1}^{3}{\mathbb{E}}\big[({\mathbf{x}}_{l}^{\top}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau})^{4}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big]^{1/2}{\mathbb{E}}\big[(\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}V_{n}{\mathbf{x}}_{m})^{4}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big]^{1/2}.

(D.37)

Note that
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}={\mathcal{B}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\subseteq{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}

so that
1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\leq 1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}
. We can then condition on
R_{k}
and
apply (
a
) of Lemma
4
to
the first quadratic form in (
D.36
) to get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|{\mathbf{x}}_{l}^{\top}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}|^{4}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}\left|\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{z}}_{l,[1:T]}\\
\bm{\epsilon}_{l,[1:T]}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{l}\Phi_{l}\\
I_{T}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{T-\tau}\\
{\mathbf{0}}_{\tau\times(T-\tau)}\end{pmatrix}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\right|^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathbb{E}}\big[\mathrm{tr}(R_{k}^{2}(\sigma_{l}^{2}\Phi_{l}^{\top}\Phi_{l}+I_{T}))^{2}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{k}}\big]=O(\sigma_{l}^{4}T^{2}),

where the last equality follows from using
tr(R)\leq T\lVert{R}\rVert
and applying Lemma
3
. Similarly, for the quadratic involving
V_{1}
in (
D.37
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}V_{1}{\mathbf{x}}_{m}|^{4}={\mathbb{E}}|\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}_{m}|^{4}\lesssim\mathrm{tr}(\sigma_{m}^{2}\Phi_{m}^{\top}\Phi_{m}+I_{T})^{2}=O(\sigma_{m}^{4}T^{2}).

We observe here that that the matrices
V_{2}
and
V_{3}
are smaller in magnitude in comparison to
V_{1}
by a factor of
\theta^{-1}
under the event
{\mathcal{B}}_{2}
. Hence it is to be expected that the quadratic forms involving
V_{2}
and
V_{3}
in (
D.37
) should be negligible in comparison to the one involving
V_{1}
. To be more concrete, we sketch here how bound the quadratic form involving
V_{2}
; the case of
V_{3}
can be dealt with in a similar manner.
Recall that the matrix
E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}
can be written as
E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}=E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0}+\frac{1}{T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}
.
Then we can write

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}

\displaystyle V_{2}{\mathbf{x}}_{m}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}|^{4}=\frac{1}{\theta^{4}}{\mathbb{E}}\big|\underline{\beta}_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{0}^{\top}E_{0}R_{k}E_{\tau}^{\top}E_{\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{m}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big|^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{\theta^{4}}{\mathbb{E}}\big|\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0}R_{k}E_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k\tau}{\mathbf{x}}_{m}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big|^{4}+\frac{1}{\theta^{4}}{\mathbb{E}}\big|\frac{1}{T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\frac{1}{T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}_{m}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big|^{4}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\theta^{4}}{\mathbb{E}}\big|\frac{1}{T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0}R_{k}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}_{m}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big|^{4}+\frac{1}{\theta^{4}}{\mathbb{E}}\big|\frac{1}{T}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k\tau}^{\top}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}\underline{\bm{\epsilon}}_{k0}^{\top}R_{k}E_{k0}^{\top}E_{k0}{\mathbf{x}}_{m}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big|^{4}.

At this point we recognize that the four terms above has a similar structure as the case of
V_{1}
. Namely they all involve quadratic forms where the matrix in the middle is independent from the vectors on each side. Using the same approach as we did in the case of
V_{1}
we can indeed show that this is a negligible term in comparison. The case of
V_{3}
is similar albeit more tedious, and we omit the details.
We may then conclude that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|\frac{1}{\theta}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}

\displaystyle\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}X_{0}VX_{0}^{\top}\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}\big|^{2}\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\theta^{2}T^{4}}\sum_{l=1}^{K}\sum_{m=1}^{K}\sigma_{l}^{2}\sigma_{m}^{2}T^{2}.

(D.38)

The same strategy described above can then be repeated for each of the remaining three terms in (
D.32
) to show that they are higher order terms compared to the term in (
D.38
) (see the remark below (
D.32
)).
We may therefore conclude that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}|{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}(Q^{-1}-\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}){\mathbf{e}}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}|^{2}\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\theta^{2}T^{2}}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{K}\sigma_{m}^{2}\right)^{2}.

(D.39)

Finally, we can decompose
\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]
into

\displaystyle\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{p}({\mathbb{E}}_{i}-{\mathbb{E}}_{i-1}){\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{p}({\mathbb{E}}_{i}-{\mathbb{E}}_{i-1}){\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}(Q^{-1}-Q^{-1}_{(kk)}){\mathbf{e}}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}},

where for the last equality we refer to (
D.33
) and the remark immediately below it. Using the bound (
D.39
) we immediately have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\big|\underline{{\mathbb{E}}}[

\displaystyle{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]-{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}Q^{-1}{\mathbf{e}}_{i}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}]\big|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\sum_{k=1}^{p}{\mathbb{E}}|{\mathbf{e}}_{i}^{\top}(Q^{-1}-\underline{Q}_{(kk)}^{-1}1_{{\mathcal{B}}_{2}}){\mathbf{e}}_{i}|^{2}\lesssim\frac{K^{2}}{\theta^{2}T}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{K}\sigma_{m}^{2}\right)^{2},

from which the claim follows.
∎

Appendix E
Proof of Theorem
4

Proof of Theorem
4
.

Without loss of generality, we only consider the case for
Z_{i,\tau}>0
since the case for
Z_{i,\tau}<0
can be considered in precisely the same way. For a constant significant level
\alpha
, to see
Pr(Z_{i,\tau}>z_{\alpha}|H_{1})\rightarrow 1
as
T,p\to\infty
, it is sufficient to show that
Z_{i,\tau}\rightarrow\infty
as
T,p\to\infty
.

To start, we firstly notice that for any
i\in\{1,2,...,K\}
and a finite time lag
\tau
,
\frac{\gamma_{i,\tau}}{2\sqrt{2}v_{i,\tau}}
does not divergent with
T
and
p
, since both
\gamma_{i,\tau}
and
v_{i,\tau}
are some constants when
T,p\to\infty
. It then suffices to show
\sqrt{T}\frac{\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(1)}-\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(2)}}{\theta_{i,\tau}}\to\infty
when
T,p\to\infty
. Note that by the definition of
\theta_{i,\tau}
in (
4.4
), we can show that

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(1)}-\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(2)}}{\theta_{i,\tau}}=\frac{\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(1)}}{\theta_{i,\tau}^{(1)}}\frac{\theta_{i,\tau}^{(1)}}{\theta_{i,\tau}}-\frac{\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(2)}}{\theta_{i,\tau}^{(2)}}\frac{\theta_{i,\tau}^{(2)}}{\theta_{i,\tau}}=\frac{\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(1)}}{\theta_{i,\tau}^{(1)}}\frac{2+2c}{2+c}-\frac{\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(2)}}{\theta_{i,\tau}^{(2)}}\frac{2}{2+c},

(E.1)

where the second equation follows from the fact that
\theta_{i,\tau}^{(1)}=(1+c)\theta_{i,\tau}^{(2)}
and
\theta_{i,\tau}=\frac{\theta_{i,\tau}^{(1)}+\theta_{i,\tau}^{(2)}}{2}=\frac{2+c}{2}\theta_{i,\tau}^{(2)}
. Moreover, under Assumptions
1
and
2
, we know from Theorem
2
that for
m=1
and
2
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}\frac{\gamma_{i,\tau}^{(m)}}{2v_{i,\tau}^{(m)}}\frac{\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(m)}-\theta_{i,\tau}^{(m)}}{\theta_{i,\tau}^{(m)}}\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}(0,1),

as
T,p\to\infty
where
\theta_{i,\tau}^{(m)}
is the asymptotic centering of
\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(m)}
. As a result,

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(m)}}{\theta_{i,\tau}^{(m)}}=1+o_{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right),

as
T,p\to\infty
, where we stress the fact that
\gamma_{i,\tau}^{(m)}
and
v_{i,\tau}^{(m)}
are constant when
T,p\to\infty
.
Therefore, (
E.1
) reduces to

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(1)}-\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(2)}}{\theta_{i,\tau}}=\frac{2+2c}{2+c}\left(1+o_{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right)\right)-\frac{2}{2+c}\left(1+o_{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right)\right)=\frac{2c}{2+c}+o_{P}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\frac{2c}{2+c}\right),

for
T,p\to\infty
, and we conclude that

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}\frac{\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(1)}-\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(2)}}{\theta_{i,\tau}}=\sqrt{T}\frac{2c}{2+c}+o_{P}\left(\frac{2c}{2+c}\right),

when
T,p\to\infty
.

Consequently, when
T,p\to\infty
,
Z_{i,\tau}\rightarrow\infty
as long as
\sqrt{T}\frac{2c}{2+c}\to\infty
and
\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(1)}\neq\lambda_{i,\tau}^{(2)}
. And it is sufficient to show the assertion in this theorem.
∎

Appendix F
Additional Simulations on the Power of Autocovariance Test

F.1
The Impact of Autocorrelation on the Power of Autocovariance Test

In this section, we study the impact of the autocorrelations on the power of autocovariance test. To be specific, we consider exactly the same setup as in Section
5
of the main paper, except that we keep the variance of factors the same across two different factor models but
set the AR coefficients for the second population to be

\phi_{1}^{(2)}=0.9\phi_{1}^{(1)},0.8\phi_{1}^{(1)},0.7\phi_{1}^{(1)},0.6\phi_{1}^{(1)},0.5\phi_{1}^{(1)},
respectively.
By doing that, we can investigate how the empirical powers of the autocovariance test are affected by the difference between autocorrelations of factors in two factor models.

Similar to Section
5
, for each combination of
T,N
and
\delta
, two high-dimensional time series observations are generated first. We then follow the same estimation and testing procedures in Section
4.2
and compute the test statistic
\widetilde{Z}_{i,\tau}
by (
4.9
), where again
B=500
bootstrap samples are generated to find
\widetilde{\theta}_{i,\tau}^{(m)\ast}
,
\widetilde{v}_{i,\tau}^{(m)\ast}
, and
\widetilde{\gamma}_{i,\tau}^{(m)\ast}
for both samples with the number of factors assumed to be known (i.e.,
\widetilde{K}_{m}=1
). Lastly, based on
M=500
Monte Carlo simulations, the empirical powers of a one-sided autocovariance test for
i=1,

\tau=1
, and
\alpha=0.1
can be estimated by the empirical probability that
\widetilde{Z}_{1,1}
is greater than
z_{1-\alpha}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbf{1}_{\left\{\widetilde{Z}_{1,1}(m)>z_{1-\alpha}\right\}},

where we have assumed
\mu_{1,1}^{(1)}>\mu_{1,1}^{(2)}
for various choices of
\phi_{1}^{(2)}
.

Figure 7
:
Empirical powers of the autocovariance test with
T=400
,
N=200,400,800,
and
\delta=0,0.1,0.3,0.5.

Figure 8
:
Empirical powers of the autocovariance test with
T=800
,
N=200,400,800,
and
\delta=0,0.1,0.3,0.5.

As presented in Figure
7
and
8
, for all ratios of
N
and
T
, empirical powers increase towards
1
, while
\phi_{1}^{(2)}
drops from
0.9\phi_{1}^{(1)}
to
0.5\phi_{1}^{(1)}
. As a consequence, Figure
7
and
8
suggest that the autocovariance test can correctly reject the null hypothesis when two high-dimensional time series have different temporal autocorrelations
\phi_{1}^{(2)}\neq\phi_{1}^{(1)}
. However, unlike the case for the impact of variance, empirical powers of the one-sided autocovariance test for relatively weak factor models with large
\delta
, especially
\delta=0.5
, are slightly lower than those of relatively strong factor models with small
\delta
. In other words, compared with strong factor models (i.e. high factor strength), the autocovariance test for weak factor models is slightly less potent in detecting the same proportional changes in autocorrelations of factors for two different factor models.

F.2
The Impact of Temporal-dependent Noises on the Power of Autocovariance Test

In this section, we study the impact of temporal-dependent noises on the power of autocovariance test. To be specific, we consider exactly the same setup as in Section
5
of the main paper, except that we change the DGP of
\{\epsilon_{j,t}\}
from i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
to the same AR(1) processes as
\{f_{1,t}^{(m)}\}
but with various variances. In that sense, the error components are temporal-dependent and we are studying the case of “
1
large spikes
+(N-1)
small/medium eigenvalues” in the symmetrized autocovariance matrix. To explore the impact of temporal-dependent noise, we set the standard deviation of
\{\epsilon_{j,t}\}
to be
1,2
and
5
, respectively.

Figure 9
:
Empirical powers of the autocovariance test with
T=400
,
N=200,400,800,
and
\sigma_{\epsilon}=0,1,2,5.

Empirical powers of the autocovariance test with
T=400
and various choices of
N
are presented in Figure
9
. When the autocovariances of noise components are relatively small (
\sigma_{\epsilon}=1,2
), i.e. in the case of “
1
large spikes
+(N-1)
small eigenvalues” in the symmetrized autocovariance matrix, the power of autocovariance test is not much different from the case with i.i.d. errors. However, when the autocovariance of noise increases (
\sigma_{\epsilon}=5
), i.e. in the case of “
1
large spikes
+(N-1)
medium eigenvalues”, the power drops since the estimation of sample autocovariance matrices starts to suffer from the “curse of dimensionality”.

F.3
The Impact of Time Lags on the Power of Autocovariance Test

In this section, we study the impact of the choice of time lags on the power of autocovariance test. To be specific, we consider exactly the same setup as in Section
5
of the main paper, except that we perform our test on various choices of time lag
\tau
. To explore the impact of time lag
\tau
, we perform the test for
\tau=1,2,3
and
5
, respectively.

Figure 10
:
Empirical powers of the autocovariance test with
T=400
,
N=200,400,800,
and
\tau=1,2,3,5.

Empirical powers of the autocovariance test with
T=400
and various choices of
N
are presented in Figure
10
. When the time lag
\tau
increases, the power of the autocovariance test drops. This is mainly because when
\tau
is relatively large, the autocorrelation of the factors, which are generated with AR(
1
) and
\phi_{1}=0.5
, becomes weak so that the spikiness of the first eigenvalue drops accordingly.

Appendix G
Additional Study on Hierarchical Clustering for Multi-country Mortality Data

G.1
Comparing Projection Matrices for Multi-country Mortality Data with One Factor

As discussed in Remark
4
, the test of eigenvectors and eigenspace is of interest to many applications as discussed by a few works such as
Fan
et al. 2022
and
Silin and
Fan 2020
, and we notice that
Tang
et al. 2022
proposed a clustering method for multi-country mortality data using eigen functions under a functional time series setup.

Therefore, in this section, we include a comparison of the mortality rate projection matrices for countries with one spiked factor to study the similarity of the mortality data.

For each country, the projection matrix is computed as the sum of eigenvectors corresponding to spiked eigenvalues multiplied by their transpose, i.e.
\sum_{i}^{K}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top}
, where
v_{i}
is the eigenvector corresponding to the
i
-th largest eigenvalue of the symmetrized autocovariance matrix. We measure the dissimilarity between each pair of countries by the
\ell_{2}
norm of the difference between two projection matrices. The
\ell_{2}
norm takes values between
0
(for exactly the same projection matrix) and
1
(for projections corresponding to orthonormal eigenvectors). The results are presented in Figure
11
.

Figure 11
:
\ell_{2}
norm of the difference between the projection matrices for countries with one factor

Indeed, for most countries (except for Bulgaria) with one factor, the dissimilarity is relatively small. The similarity in projection matrices reveals that common human characteristics can be extracted from different countries. Meanwhile, this similarity does not guarantee that common human characteristics are of the same importance in leading the trend of human mortality rates in different countries. This motivates our proposed test on the equivalence of the largest eigenvalues since it can tell whether common human characteristics (compared to the country-specific features such as socioeconomic conditions) are of the same strength in affecting the mortality rates in different countries. Similar discussions on the roles of both human characteristics and country-specific features in mortality forecasting are also seen in
Li and Lee 2005
.

G.2
Hierarchical Clustering for Multi-country Mortality Data with More than One Factors

For countries with more than one factor, to test on the equivalence of autocovariances through factor models, test statistics between each pair of countries are computed for all three factors as
\widetilde{Z}_{1,1},\widetilde{Z}_{2,1}
and
\widetilde{Z}_{3,1}
. As depicted in Figure
12
, the
p
-values for
\widetilde{Z}_{1,1}
and
\widetilde{Z}_{2,1}
between all pairs of countries are relatively large, which suggests that the differences of the first two factors between each pair of countries are not significant (at
\alpha=0.1
). Nonetheless,
p
-values for
\widetilde{Z}_{1,3}
are relatively small between Canada and France, Canada and Italy, and Italy and Portugal.
As a result, despite that
p
-value is
0.09
for
\widetilde{Z}_{1,3}
between Italy and Portugal, one may suggest considering France, Italy, and Portugal have similar spiked eigenvalues of their autocovariance matrices in a three-factor model and include them in a combined statistical analysis while leaving Canada for an independent analysis.
To measure the dissimilarity of mortality data between two countries with more than one factors, the overall distance between two countries can be defined as a weighted average of the distances for all factors. In specific, for each pair of countries, we can define the distance for the
i
-th factor as
dist_{i}=1-p_{i}
, where
p_{i}
is the
p
-value of the autocovariance test computed using the
i
-th factor of both countries. It is then straightforward to compute the overall distance between this pair of countries as

dist=\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}\cdot dist_{i},

where
w_{i}
is a weight on
dist_{i}
. Practically, we suggest that the weight
w_{i}
is related to the magnitude of each singular value of the autocovariance matrix (or equivalently the squared root of the eigenvalues of symmetrized autocovariance matrix), since the singular values are related to the autocovariance explained by each factor. Based on this idea, we compute
w_{i}
as

w_{i}=(w_{i}^{(1)}+w_{i}^{(2)})/2,

where
w_{i}^{(1)}=\sqrt{\tilde{\lambda}_{i,\tau}^{(1)}}/\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\sqrt{\tilde{\lambda}_{i,\tau}^{(1)}}\right)
and
w_{i}^{(2)}=\sqrt{\tilde{\lambda}_{i,\tau}^{(2)}}/\left(\sum_{i=1}^{K}\sqrt{\tilde{\lambda}_{i,\tau}^{(2)}}\right)
. The result of hierarchical clustering using average linkage for all countries with three factors is presented in Figure
13
.

Figure 12
:
p
-values of the autocovariance test for each pair of countries that have three factors in the estimated factor model

Figure 13
:
Cluster dendrogram for countries that have three factors in the estimated factor model

However, as discussed in Remark
4
, even the number of factors across some countries are different, it may still be of interest to perform the test for the first factor only. The idea behind is also very straightforward, that is we can study whether the mortality rates across all countries have the same low-dimensional representations in the eigenspace spanned by the first eigenvector shared by all countries. In this sense, we perform autocovariance tests and the hierarchical clustering analysis on the first factor for all countries regardless of the estimated total number of factors. The result of
p
-values computed for the first factors are illustrated in Figure
14
and the result of hierarchical clustering using average linkage for the first factor is presented in Figure
15
.
As seen in Figure
14
, in addition to what has been discussed for those countries with only one factor in their factor models, the first factor of Portugal and France are also different from the first factor of Bulgaria and Finland, respectively. Consequently, despite the differences between the estimated numbers of factors for Canada, Denmark, Italy, Poland, and all other countries, the total death rates projected in the eigenspace spanned by the first common eigenvector are not significantly different across these countries.

:
p
-values of the autocovariance test of the first factor for all countries

Figure 15
:
Cluster dendrogram for all countries using the first factor

Appendix H
Simulation Studies on Gaussian Assumption in Theorem
2

In this section, we conduct simulations to show that the results of Theorem
2
can still be obtained when we replace the Gaussian error
\epsilon_{it}
by Gamma or Student’s t distributed errors. The simulations considered in this section are based on the following factor models

\displaystyle{\mathbf{y}}_{t}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{diag}(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{K})\\
\bm{0}_{p\times K}\end{pmatrix}{\mathbf{f}}_{t}+\bm{\epsilon}_{t}

(H.1)

where we choose
T=4000,\ p=400,\ K=2
,
\sigma_{1}=50
,
\sigma_{2}=10
, and the factors
{\mathbf{f}}_{t}
are generated by MA(
2
) processes

\displaystyle f_{it}=\sum_{l=0}^{2}\phi_{il}z_{i,t-l},\quad i=1,2,\quad t=1,\ldots,T,

where
\bm{\phi}_{1}=(5,3,1)
and
\bm{\phi}_{2}=(1,-2,1)
with variances of factors normalized to 1. The errors
\bm{\epsilon}_{t}
are generated i.i.d. from three different distributions:(1) Gaussian i.e.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
; (2) Gamma
(2,2)
with mean and variance normalized to 0 and 1, respectively; (3) Student’s t with d.f.
=4
with mean and variance normalized to 0 and 1, respectively. By generating
M=5000
Monte Carlo simulations, we draw the histograms and density plots of
\lambda_{1,1}
and
\lambda_{2,1}
for the above three cases (subject to the same scaling) as shown in Figure
16
to
17
, where the differences among the histograms and density plots are rather small.

Figure 16
:
Histograms of
\lambda_{1,1}
and
\lambda_{2,1}
(subject to a rescaling) under three different assumptions of
\bm{\epsilon}_{t}

Figure 17
:
Density plots of
\lambda_{1,1}
and
\lambda_{2,1}
(subject to a rescaling) under three different assumptions of
\bm{\epsilon}_{t}
